Um, no, I wouldn't say I need to control *everything*. I mean, I like things to be organized and for plans to go smoothly, but I think that's just being responsible, right?

I really try to just go with the flow when things don't pan out exactly how I expected in my personal life, too. I don't want you to think I'm some sort of control freak who falls apart if a dinner reservation gets canceled or something. I try to be very easygoing about that kind of stuff.

I hope I'm answering this the right way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need and not making it sound like I'm overly rigid or difficult to be around.

Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, sleep is important, obviously, but I don't lay awake worrying about *not* sleeping. That sounds like it would just make it worse, right?

I'm really trying to be helpful here.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried specifically about not sleeping well, even on nights when work isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, sleep is important, obviously, but I don't lay awake worrying about *not* sleeping. That sounds like it would just make it worse, right? \n\nIf I'm awake, it's usually just because I'm naturally a little restless or maybe I had a late cup of coffee. I definitely don't have this, like, anxiety about the act of sleeping itself. I try to practice good sleep hygiene and just let my body rest when it's ready. I'm pretty sensible about it.\n\nI wouldn't want you to think I'm making a mountain out of a molehill when it comes to bedtime. I really try to just keep things normal and manageable. Am I explaining that okay? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of how I handle things.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel anxious or worried about things that might happen in the future, even when there's no clear reason to believe they will?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 48 / financial_or_investment_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, not really. I mean, I think about the future, of course, because I have to plan for things—especially with my job, you're always looking ahead at projections and stuff. But I wouldn't call it worrying or being anxious about things for no reason. 

I try to be really rational about what's coming up. I don't sit around imagining worst-case scenarios or anything like that. I feel like that would just be a waste of energy, you know? I really try to stay positive and just deal with things as they come, rather than borrowing trouble. 

I definitely don't want to sound like a pessimist or someone who's always anticipating disaster. I really try to keep a good perspective on things. Does that make sense? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers... I just want to make sure I'm doing this right for you.

I mean, to sometimes just feel a bit tense even when there isn't a big deadline? I hope I'm not overcomplicating things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of different things, even when there isn't a clear reason or specific problem to focus on?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I try not to be one of those people who just worries about everything under the sun, you know? I really do try to focus on the positives. \n\nI guess... well, sometimes my mind might wander a bit to things like, I don't know, if the house is organized or if I'm keeping up with my friends enough, but I wouldn't call it worrying exactly. It's more just... being responsible, I think? I definitely don't want you to think I'm just sitting around agonizing over every little thing in my life. I really do keep it together pretty well most of the time. \n\nDoes that sound like the right track? I just want to make sure I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't control the worrying, even when you try to stop thinking about work or other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I really wouldn't say it's out of control. I mean, I definitely don't want to sound like I'm just spiraling or anything like that. \n\nIf I really need to, I can usually just... pull myself together, you know? I like to think I have a pretty good handle on things. I mean, sure, sometimes it takes a little effort to switch gears and put the firm out of my mind for the evening, but I wouldn't call it uncontrollable. I'm still very capable of managing myself and my responsibilities. \n\nI hope that's an okay answer? I just really don't want to give you the wrong idea about how I'm functioning. Am I making sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling noticeably more anxious or worried than other people in similar situations, even about things that most might consider minor or routine?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really think so. I mean, I try to keep things in perspective. I definitely don't want to come across as overly dramatic or anything. \n\nI think I handle things pretty much the same way anyone else in my position would. Maybe I'm a little thorough, but I wouldn't say I'm noticeably more anxious than other people. I really try to be resilient and just deal with whatever comes up without making a fuss about it. \n\nI hope that's what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture. I'm really trying to be helpful.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling unusually tired or low on energy during the day, even after trying to rest?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say unusually tired. I mean, I work full time at the firm, and that's naturally going to take a lot out of anyone, right? I try to keep my energy up as best I can. \n\nI definitely don't want to sound like I'm just dragging myself around all day or being lazy. I make sure I get my work done and keep up with everything I need to do. Sometimes I might feel a little drained by the late afternoon, but I think that's just normal for someone my age with a demanding job. I really try to push through it. \n\nIs that the kind of thing you're looking for? I just want to make sure I'm not making it sound worse than it is. I'm really trying to be accurate for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often experience physical symptoms like the stomach tightness you mentioned even when you're not particularly stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I mean, the stomach tightness really only happens when things are getting a bit intense at work, like before a big presentation or something. \n\nI wouldn't say it just happens out of nowhere. I really try to keep myself in check, and I definitely don't want you to think my body is just reacting to nothing. I think it's a pretty normal response to a high-pressure environment, right? I mean, anyone would feel a little knot in their stomach when there's a lot on the line. \n\nI really hope I'm answering this right. I just want to be sure I'm giving you the information you need without making it sound like I'm falling apart or anything. Am I doing okay with these questions?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself getting anxious or worried specifically about not being able to sleep, even before you go to bed?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I try not to let myself get worked up about sleep. That seems like it would just make things worse, right? \n\nI really try to just have a normal nighttime routine and wind down like anyone else. I definitely don't want to sound like I'm obsessing over it or laying there panicking about not sleeping before I even get into bed. If I have a restless night here and there, I just figure it's part of being a busy professional. I try to just take it in stride.\n\nI hope that makes sense? I'm really trying to give you the right idea of how I handle things. I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and not overcomplicating everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about small or routine things, even when you know logically that they probably aren’t a big deal?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I worry about small things. I mean, I'm an analyst, so my job is literally about paying attention to details and managing risks. It's just part of how I'm wired to, you know, be thorough and prepared. \n\nI definitely don't want you to think I'm sitting around fretting over every little thing or making mountains out of molehills. I think I handle the day-to-day stuff pretty well, just like anyone else trying to keep their life organized. If I double-check something, it's just to be responsible, not because I'm irrationally worried about it. \n\nIs that what you mean? I just want to make sure I'm not giving you the impression that I'm overly anxious about normal, everyday things. I'm really trying to give you a clear picture of how I operate.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel anxious or have physical symptoms like stomach tightness even when there isn’t any particular work pressure or external stressor happening?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. When there's no pressure, like on a quiet weekend, I'm usually just... fine. I really try to leave work at work and enjoy my downtime, you know? \n\nI wouldn't say I get that stomach tightness for absolutely no reason. That sounds like I'm just kind of... falling apart out of nowhere, and I promise I'm not doing that. I really try to keep a good handle on things. If I do feel a little tense on a day off, it's usually just because I'm mentally prepping for the week ahead or thinking through a client's portfolio, which is pretty standard for my field. But not just out of the blue. \n\nDoes that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you accurate answers and being a good patient. I really don't want to give you the wrong impression about how I cope with things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a strong need to be in control of everything in your life, to the point where it causes you distress if things aren’t going exactly as planned?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I need to control *everything*. I mean, I like things to be organized and for plans to go smoothly, but I think that's just being responsible, right? \n\nI definitely don't get distressed or upset if things change. I'm pretty adaptable. In my line of work, the markets are always shifting, so you have to be able to pivot. I really try to just go with the flow when things don't pan out exactly how I expected in my personal life, too. I don't want you to think I'm some sort of control freak who falls apart if a dinner reservation gets canceled or something. I try to be very easygoing about that kind of stuff.\n\nI hope I'm answering this the right way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need and not making it sound like I'm overly rigid or difficult to be around.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling anxious or worried about a number of different everyday things, even when you know there may not be a clear reason to be concerned about them?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I worry about a bunch of different things without a reason. Like I mentioned, if I'm thinking about something, it's usually tied to work or, you know, making sure my life is running smoothly. \n\nI definitely don't want to come across like a worrier who just sits around spinning her wheels over nothing. I try really hard to stay grounded and focus on what's actually in front of me. I mean, everyone has passing thoughts, right? But I don't let them take over or get anxious over everyday stuff that doesn't matter. I really try to just handle my business and keep moving forward. \n\nIs that helpful? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples and not sounding like I'm anxious all the time. I'm really trying to be helpful here.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried specifically about not sleeping well, even on nights when work isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, sleep is important, obviously, but I don't lay awake worrying about *not* sleeping. That sounds like it would just make it worse, right? \n\nIf I'm awake, it's usually just because I'm naturally a little restless or maybe I had a late cup of coffee. I definitely don't have this, like, anxiety about the act of sleeping itself. I try to practice good sleep hygiene and just let my body rest when it's ready. I'm pretty sensible about it.\n\nI wouldn't want you to think I'm making a mountain out of a molehill when it comes to bedtime. I really try to just keep things normal and manageable. Am I explaining that okay? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right picture of how I handle things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things that might happen in the future, even when there's no clear reason to believe they will?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I think about the future, of course, because I have to plan for things—especially with my job, you're always looking ahead at projections and stuff. But I wouldn't call it worrying or being anxious about things for no reason. \n\nI try to be really rational about what's coming up. I don't sit around imagining worst-case scenarios or anything like that. I feel like that would just be a waste of energy, you know? I really try to stay positive and just deal with things as they come, rather than borrowing trouble. \n\nI definitely don't want to sound like a pessimist or someone who's always anticipating disaster. I really try to keep a good perspective on things. Does that make sense? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers... I just want to make sure I'm doing this right for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling anxious or restless even when there’s nothing specific on your mind or no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I feel restless or anxious just out of the blue like that. I mean, I like to stay busy, and sometimes if I have a little downtime, I might feel like I should be doing something productive, you know? But I wouldn't call it anxiety. \n\nI really try to keep a level head and not let myself get worked up over nothing. I think it's important to be able to just relax when you can, and I try to do that. I definitely wouldn't want you to think I'm just sitting around vibrating with nervous energy for no reason. I try to be very steady. \n\nIs that the kind of thing you're asking about? I just want to make sure I'm not misunderstanding the question and giving you useless information. I really want to be as helpful as I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about things outside of work, like personal relationships, health, or other aspects of your life, even when you're not actively thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I wouldn't say that. If a friend needs me, I'm there, and if I need to go to the doctor for a checkup, I go, but I don't sit around spinning my wheels worrying about what *could* go wrong. I think it's really important to just maintain good, stable relationships and not create unnecessary drama, you know? \n\nI definitely don't want to be one of those people who is always complaining or stressing out everyone around them. I really try to be a steady presence for the people in my life. I like to think I handle things pretty gracefully. \n\nIs that helpful? I just want to make sure I'm answering these the way you need me to, and that I'm not leaving out anything important you're looking for.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
